*A thousand apart in booleans (2026-10-17, `geom/src/intersect/field.rs` › `Pieces`, `march.rs` › `rescaled`, `between`, `geom/src/nurbs.rs` › `NurbsSurface::evened`, `boolean/freeform.rs` › `settled_sigma`, `finer`, `evened`, `topo/src/mass.rs` › `net_change`).* The two causes above were confirmed, and behind them three more; each has a general method, and each applies to every freeform surface (a polynomial one included), where it changes nothing but cost unless the old bound was the one in the way.

- *The marcher's cells take the bounds of the pieces under them.* A freeform chart keeps its derivative bounds piece by piece (`Pieces`): from each Bézier span, a piece is split where a split tightens a bound on a first derivative by more than half again (`PIECE_GAIN` 1.5, the gain of halves along u, along v, or of quarters per halving), the pieces that gain most first (a heap, so that the budget of 8192 pieces goes next to the light end of a span rather than evenly; breadth first it ran out on the rod skewed ten thousandfold along its axis, and halves alone missed the plate's heavy corner, where only quarters gain). A node holds the larger of its leaves' bounds where that is tighter than its own. `Chart::bounds_in` takes the largest bounds of the pieces that meet a cell, and the bounds of the whole surface where those are tighter or the cell reaches past the domain. The grid, the floor cell and the borders keep the bounds of the whole surface. Every piece's bound is a proof over it, so the rule for splitting (its gain, its budget) decides only what a cell costs, never whether a bound holds. The pieces of a surface are kept on a shelf of 32 surfaces, as the projector is: on the benchmark the turbofan's 1312 charts took 0.93 s of pieces made anew and take 0.08 s, the impeller's 714 0.58 s and 0.06 s.
- *A step across a jump in the parametrisation's speed* (`Marcher::rescaled`). Where two spans of a spline meet and are drawn at paces a hundred apart (the twins skewed along u: each span is reparametrised on its own), the step predicted from the derivatives on one side goes a hundred times too far or too short however short it is made, and the march stalled at the knot. Where halving has run out, the parameter step is scaled to the length it went and, once one went too short and one too far, halved between the two (48 times at most), until a step lands within half to twice its length, ahead, turning less than the march allows, on the field and in the chart. A rescaled step is taken only where the middle of its parameters corrects onto the curve near its chord (the parameters may have moved by orders of magnitude, and Newton could land on a branch close by), and a loop that closes within it closes there, by the same test as after any other step (review, 2026-10-17: without it a loop could be marched around twice; `meet_fixes.rs` › `a_circle_across_a_knot_where_the_speed_jumps_is_marched_once`). A point between two of a branch (`between`) whose middle in parameters is next to one of them, or from which Newton does not settle, starts from the foot of the middle of the chord instead, and is taken only within half the chord of its middle. All of these apply only where the old rule had given up, so every curve marched before is marched as before.
- *The winding numbers' bound is settled where it is slack, and asked locally.* A leaf whose bound on the least singular value is more than four times (`SIGMA_SLACK`) below the least of the values measured at its corners and its middle is halved, the way that raises the lower half more, until it is not (256 pieces a leaf at most, 16 halvings each way), when the tree is built (`settled_sigma`): with the weights ten thousand apart along a span, a leaf next to the light end had no bound at all (the normal is bounded through the cube of the heaviest weight, a factor of 3e7 within the leaf) and the surface no parameter speed ("stalls or folds"). And `sigma_near` asks a leaf's pieces (`finer`), lowest bound first: the lowest is split, and the split kept, where its box is more than four times the reach across (a narrow fan of iso-curves from a heavy corner has a wide box: next to the plate's light corner every leaf's box reached a boundary point it is nowhere near, and the bound there was 2000 times below the truth), or where its bound is four times below the largest value measured on it (the least, for a piece wholly within reach); the first that cannot be split is the answer. A split kept from an earlier question is looked into only where this question would make it, and the store (16,384 pieces a surface, about 11 MB for a bicubic one) is emptied when full, so the answer is a function of the question alone, whatever was asked before (rule 5: `freeform.rs` › `the_bound_near_a_point_does_not_depend_on_what_was_asked_before`). As with the marcher's pieces, `SIGMA_SLACK` and `FINE_REACH` decide how much is split, not whether the bound holds. The plate a thousand apart with a ball at its light corner: 9 s and 240,000 steps with a slack rule alone, 80 ms and 4000 steps.
- *A chart drawn evenly.* Winding numbers are of points in space, so any parametrisation of the same point set is a chart of it. A NURBS surface's chart is now that of `NurbsSurface::evened` (where it differs; its tree on the shelf of trees like any other, so that its bytes are counted): split into Bézier spans, each span of u and of v reparametrised by the Möbius map that brings the weights at its ends together (the geometric mean over every row of the net across it, so that the map is one of the parameters alone), a change of weights only; held in `nurbs.rs` › `an_evened_surface_is_the_same_surface_drawn_by_moebius_maps_span_by_span`. Where the weights have a product form (every surface of revolution; the twins skewed span by span) that is as even as the weights allow; the plate (`w₀₀ w₁₁ / (w₀₁ w₁₀) = 1 / h²`) is left as it is. Without it a boundary running along a knot between spans drawn a thousand times apart took the bound of the fast side: the frustum skewed a thousandfold both ways took 20 to 40 s a boolean (25,000 steps a stretch), now 0.1 to 1.5 s.
- *The cap of a span* (`MAX_WEIGHT_RATIO`) is two million, not a thousand: what is held, 1.4e6 (the twins a thousandfold both ways); refused by the cap: 1.4e8. The booleans and the measure part here: a boolean takes weights up to that cap within a span, `topo::mass_properties` refuses weights further apart than 1e4 after its Möbius evening (its sums settled wrong from 1e7, [`topology.md`](topology.md) › *Rational weights far apart*), so a boolean of a face skewed beyond what evening removes may be built and its result not measured.
- *The measure* (`topo::mass_properties`), for the plate's quarter disc cut at a heavy corner a thousand apart: the plan decided from the coarsest quadrature of `∮ dv` that the boundary "goes around in v" (its parameters move so unevenly there that the sum was off by more than the domain); how far the loops go around is now taken from where the samples are (`net_change`), unless they jump across a parameter whose closure is not known on a surface whose two borders there are not proven further apart, as point sets, than twice the distance a boundary point may be off its surface (`certify::curves_apart`: a seam the closure test may have missed; derived, and erring only towards the old rate sum or a refusal; a quick excursion over an unseen seam and back between two sampled places is the known limit): then the sum of the rates, as before, and a refusal where the inner integrals run along that parameter. See [`topology.md`](topology.md). And the face needed a little more than the budget (`BUDGET` 2^27, was 2^26; it settles in 0.1 s).

Measured (release, the whole set of `boolean_rational.rs`'s twins against the five tools of each, three ops, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, weights skewed along u, along v and both): ratio 10, 100 and 1000, 1620 of 1620 booleans agree with the analytic solid's within 1e-6 in volume and area (worst 9.1e-8; held in the tests with the centroid to 1e-5 of the scale, the shells and `check`); a boolean takes 0.02 to 1.5 s, a skewed group of fifteen 2 to 11 s. Ten thousand along u: all built; along v (a degree-one profile) 9 of 15 refused for the rod and 6 of 15 for the frustum at each scale ("tangent or nearly coincident over too large an area": more than a million cells), both ways refused by the cap. The plate 1, h, h, 1 with a ball at its light corner, at both heavy corners and at a corner of its bottom, three ops, at the three scales: h = 10, 100 and 1000 all built and held to the plain block's by 400 seeded points and by volume, area and centroid to 1e-9 (worst 1.9e-12); h = 1e4 refused by the winding numbers ("too close"; the measure refuses it too). Held in `boolean_rational.rs` › `a_*_with_weights_far_apart_against_tools` (two tools per ratio and direction in turn, not meshed: the tessellator's grid is even in the parameters and it does not mesh every face drawn this unevenly, open in `tess`) and `boolean.rs` › `what_is_not_supported_says_so` (h = 100 and 1000, four balls). Meshed only at ten apart in the tests: from a hundred the tessellator does not mesh every such face. *Open:* the meshes of unevenly drawn faces (in `tess`), ten thousand apart along a degree-one direction (the cells), the plate at ten thousand. (A rational face on the surface it equals, a patch or an analytic one, wholly over it or not: built since 2026-10-14 and 2026-10-15, see the table above.) (A patch with a collapsed border was refused here until 2026-10-14: see the next section.) *Cost* (the optimized test profile, four at a time on a quiet machine): a boolean with a rational quadric face takes 35 to 420 ms (medians per pair of solids 45 to 250 ms, a block the slowest tool) where the analytic twin takes 0.4 to 14 ms (closed forms against marched splines and nearest-point searches, as for any freeform face); the weighted roof against a block takes 1.5 s where the polynomial roof takes 0.2 s, because its parameters really move four times as fast and its second derivatives really are twelve times as large, and the chart (one speed for the whole face) and the proofs pay for both. The polynomial roof itself got 15 to 30 % faster by the parameter speed taken in the surface's parameters (0.28 s before).

*Meshed (2026-11-15).* What was built here and could not be shown: a face whose weights are this far apart asked the tessellator's even grid for thousands to millions of millions of cells a side. Such a face now gets a grid graded to its shape ([`tessellator.md`](tessellator.md) › *A grid graded to the shape*). Held (`crates/kernel/tests/rational_mesh.rs`): the twins drawn a thousandfold both ways against a block through their curved face, three operations each, mesh closed with every point within the tolerance of a surface of the analytic solid or of the block and the volume of the analytic boolean's within the tolerance times its area; the booleans of the block 30 × 900 × 20 with seeded weights and a block through three of its sides mesh on the closed form's boundary (they are not measured yet: [`tessellator.md`](tessellator.md) › *A grid graded to the shape*, owed 7). Still the boolean's: ten thousand apart along a degree-one direction and the plate at ten thousand refused; the chart of such a block neither counted nor bounded ([`geometry.md`](geometry.md) › *A foot that ends short*); and the curves it cuts in such a face carry knots a billionth of the size apart where the march crossed a knot of the surface, which the tessellator takes for points of the edge (triangles that fold in single precision: owed there).

**Freeform faces with a pole (2026-10-14, `geom/src/pole.rs`, `boolean/freeform.rs`, `chart.rs`).** A border of a patch that is one point is where the parameters stall: the derivative along the border is zero, `∂u × ∂v` has no length, and the parameter rectangle is no chart (a loop through the pole is not closed in it, and nothing bounds how fast the parameter along the border changes nearby). The surface itself is a surface there: smooth when it closes up smoothly (a dome), a cone-like point when it does not (a drop's tip). Every place that read the surface through its parameters is listed here with what it does now; a face without a pole takes the code it took before in each of them.

- *The chart* (`PatchTree::chart`, `chart_speed`). The parameter rectangle is drawn as the surface is. With `s` and `t` the shares of the domain along and across the poles' borders, the point goes to `(ω(t) (s − ½), t)`, with the width `ω = t` for a pole at the start, `1 − t` at the end and `t (1 − t)` for both: a wedge, or a lens between two points. A pole is one ordinary point of it, the map from the surface is one to one and continuous, and it turns the way the parameters do, so the winding number of the loops is what it is on the surface. Its speed is bounded, not sampled: a step `(ds, dt)` moves the chart by the shear `[1 k; 0 1]` of `(ω ds, dt)` with `|k| = |ω′ (s − ½)| ≤ ½` (norm at most 1.2808; 1.29 is used) and the surface by `E ω ds + F dt` with `E = S_s / ω`, `F = S_t`, so the speed is at most 1.29 over the least singular value of `[E F]`, which comes from an upper bound on `|E|² + |F|²` and a lower bound on `|E × F|` as before. `E` and `E × F` are bounded piece by piece of the tree: away from a pole by the piece's nets over the least and the greatest `ω` on it (`ω` is concave); next to a pole, where `S_s` and the normal vanish as `ω` does, by the Bézier nets of `w² S_σ` and `w³ (S_σ × S_τ)` *divided by the piece's own parameter* (`B_j^n(τ) / τ = (n / j) B_{j−1}^{n−1}(τ)`: a net whose border row is zero is the net, one degree lower, of the quotient; `geom`: `RationalBezierPatch::weighted_derivative_nets`, new, and `weighted_normal_net`). A border row counts as zero below 1e-9 of the net's longest vector (a border collapsed up to rounding is collapsed, as the measure and the detection take it). Measured, the bound is 1.4 to 2.5 times the fastest the chart is seen to move (an octant, a lune, a cone, a drop, either way round; `the_chart_of_a_surface_with_poles_moves_no_faster_than_its_speed`, which also steps to within 1e-9 of the poles).
- *The side of the surface a point is on* (`PatchTree::signed_distance`, and the marcher's field for a freeform surface, `field.rs`). The sign is that of `(p − foot) · normal`, and at a pole the formula has no normal. For a point whose nearest point is the pole the normal is taken along the meridian that leaves the pole nearest to the direction of the point (`Pole::normal_towards`): a point a little further on, whose foot is an ordinary point near the pole, has that foot on that meridian and is on the side its normal says, and the sign cannot change on the way, since it would have to pass through zero, which takes a point straight behind that meridian, for which it is the farthest in direction and not the nearest. Where the surface closes up smoothly every meridian gives the same normal. This is an argument by continuity, not a bound; it is held against the distance to a ball (straight above and below the pole of an octant, to 1e-9) and against a cone's two sides behind its apex.
- *The directions around a pole* (`outline::outward_normal`, which `regions` sorts the curves at a vertex by, `sew` and `section`). At a pole it gives the direction the surface closes around (`freeform::pole_axes`): the mean of the unit normals the surface reaches the pole with, which is its normal there when it closes up smoothly and stands in for the axis, as at a cone's apex, when it comes to a point. Seen along it the meridians leave the pole in the order they have on the surface as long as every limit normal is on the axis's side (the direction of a meridian dropped onto the plane across the axis turns at a rate with the sign of axis · normal). That is proven, not sampled: the limit normals are, up to a positive factor, the divided normal net's border row, whose vectors hold them in their hull, and each must make an angle with the axis whose cosine is above 0.05.
- *The marcher* (`geom/src/intersect/field.rs`, `short_of_poles`). The chart of a freeform surface stops a quarter of the tolerance short of a pole, as a cone's does of its apex: the border is moved in by `tolerance / (4 L)` with `L` the proven bound on the speed across it, so everything left out is within a quarter of the tolerance of the pole and a curve that runs into the pole ends within that of it, where the vertex rule joins it to the pole. The bounds the cells are judged by are upper bounds on derivatives and hold at a pole as anywhere. The grid normals of a freeform field are the limit normals at a pole.
- *Unchanged, and why:* the boxes of the tree, the bound below the distance and the nearest point need no regular parameters; `stretch_within` compares along any path in the parameters, so its proof holds (it is only less often conclusive next to a pole).

*Handled:* a pole where the surface closes up smoothly and one where it comes to a cone-like point; one pole or one at each end of the same parameter; cuts away from the pole, around it (the pole inside the tool) and through it (the pole on a face, an edge or the curved side of the tool). *Refused by name:* poles on more than two borders across both parameters (`POLES_BOTH_WAYS`; two adjacent ones are a corner since 2026-10-15, below), a point the limit normals do not all lean one way around, or folds back over (`POLE_SHAPE`, or `IRREGULAR` when the divided nets give no bound). *Held to* (`crates/kernel/tests/boolean_poles.rs`): the octant of a ball as a rational patch against the same booleans of the analytic octant, with a block, a cylinder, a ball, a cone and a torus away from the pole, around it and through it (the pole on a block's face, on a block's edge, on the side of the others), in the world's frame and tilted, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6: 288 booleans, within 3.7e-7 in volume and area (held to 1e-6), none refused since 2026-10-15 (the torus through the pole, tilted, at 1e6 was refused "imprint lost": see *The helmet's findings* below); the dome and the drop of `kernel::revolve` against the same kinds of tools by the identities of sets (1e-6) and, for planes across the axis and through it (through both poles, through the drop's point), by closed forms from the profile (1e-7). Every result passes `check` and is measured; every result is meshed, except eight kinds of cut through a pole that the tessellator declines ("segments per side") or leaves with a gap, pinned in the test's `UNMESHED` with a reproducer for `crates/tess` (meshed since the same night, §6 *Poles of spline surfaces*; the list is empty). *Cost:* 75 to 280 ms per boolean with the octant's patch (the test profile), as for any rational face.

*Cusps (2026-10-15).* A spline that leaves the axis along it sweeps a needle: its width grows as the square of the distance from the point, not in proportion, so the meridians leave the point all along one line and not in a fan. `S_s` vanishes there to the second order (the net of `w² S_σ` divides by the parameter across twice: `pole_order`), and the wedge is drawn with that width, `ω = tᵐ (1 − t)ⁿ` to the orders of its poles: `E = S_s / ω` is bounded again (the nets divided `m` times), the shear is that of `|ω′| ≤ max(m, n)` (1.634 for a cusp, 1.618 with a hundredth of margin), and a surface that closes on itself is a disc of radius `tᵐ` (speed `√(m² + 4π²)` times the tree's). The direction the surface closes around at a cusp is the line its meridians leave along, on the side its normals lean to as they leave (the next row of the divided normal's net: at a cusp the limit normals are square to the line, all around, as at the apex of a cone of no angle). Where every piece that leaves a vertex leaves it along the normal of its tangent plane (at a cusp, all of them), the regions put them in order a little further along (`untie`, all of them at once). A cusp at either end of a surface with a pole at each (an egg whose chart is a sphere) is refused (`POLE_SHAPE`). Measured: the speed bound of the wedge and of the disc against the fastest seen (`the_chart_of_a_surface_with_poles_moves_no_faster_than_its_speed`: 2.29 against 0.57; the closed charts' test: 6.58 against 0.74); `boolean_poles.rs` › `a_cusp_is_an_operand`: a needle against a block around its point, a slab away from it, a ball, a leaning block and a half-space across the axis, at three scales: closed forms from the profile where the tool cuts across the axis (1e-7), the identities of sets, 400 seeded points against the exact membership, every result meshed. (A face that holds the axis through the point was refused until 2026-10-17: next paragraph.)

*A cusp against a face that holds its axis (2026-10-17, `geom/src/pole.rs` › `narrowing`, `geom/src/intersect/field.rs` › `short_of_poles`, `Chart::bounds_in`, `march.rs` › `on_into_cusp`).* A plane through the needle's axis, the two faces of a block whose edge is the axis, or a cylinder with the axis on its side were refused "surfaces that touch along a curve": the needle is walked in its parameters, and next to the point the distance to such a face is `f ≈ ρ(t) g(θ)` with `ρ ~ c t²`, so `f` and its gradient vanish to the second order all along the border the chart stopped at (`tol / (4 L)` from the point, where the needle is `1e-14` wide), the cells there are neither empty nor simple, and the critical point found is flat. Three rules, each for any freeform surface with a pole of order two or more (`pole::narrowing`: the order is how many times the net of `w² S_σ` of every span at the pole divides by the parameter across, as `freeform::pole_order` counts it; the divided net `N` bounds `|S_σ| ≤ β^k |N| / w²`) and any other surface, and only where the other surface passes within the tolerance of the point *and holds the strip that would be left out* (`holds_the_strip`; elsewhere the chart stops as at any pole, which keeps a plane across the axis near the point cut where it is): (0) *The other surface holds the strip.* Every point of the iso-curve at `β` is within `r(β) = ½ width β^k` of the meridian `A` through the middle of the border, and the other surface's distance `F` is 1-Lipschitz; the strip is left out only where `|F(A(β))| ≤ r(β) + tol` from the tolerance around the point all the way to the border (walked by halving stretches across, each settled by its middle and the speed across, down to an eighth of the tolerance; at most 100 000 samples). And the other surface must cross every iso-curve of the strip the same number of times, from the border down to the tolerance around the point (`same_crossings`: iso-curves a tolerance apart in space across, each sampled half a tolerance apart by the bound on its length, the sign read where it is above its rounding; at most 2 000 000 samples): no piece of the intersection then begins or ends inside the strip, and every piece in it crosses the border, where the marcher finds it. (Found in the second review: within the strip's width of the meridian a cylinder or a ball of radius 1 through the point that crosses the axis again at 4e-3 meets the needle in a closed loop from 2.7e-3 to 8e-3 wholly inside the strip; the needle less it came back whole, the tip through the tool by 2 tolerances. A piece thinner than the sampling, a tolerance across, is still missed.) (Found in review: without it a ball of radius `c` about the axis through the point, swallowing the tip up to `2 c`, met the needle in nothing, and the needle less the ball came back whole with its tip through the ball, valid by `check`. The other way, `|F| > r + tol` all along, cannot hold for a surface through the point.) (1) *The chart stops where the surface is wide.* The border moves in to where every iso-curve along it is at most `CUSP_WIDTH` long by the bound: `ρ > TOUCH tol / sin φ` is where the marcher tells a crossing at the angle `φ` from a touch (`touches`: `|∇f| ≤ TOUCH tol |H|`, `TOUCH` = 4, with `|H| ≈ 1 / ρ` across a cusp), a whole turn of that is `2π TOUCH tol`, and the bound is at most four times the iso-curve (`WIDTH_SLACK`, held in `pole.rs`), so `CUSP_WIDTH = 4 · 2π · 4 tol ≈ 100 tol` for crossings at a right angle; a crossing at the border shallower than the slack allows is refused as a touch (on the needle at scale 1, `t = 1.4e-3`). At a width of 4 tolerances the cells found a "touch" where the plane crosses the needle square (gradient 0.999, Hessian 2.9e5). What is left out lies within `r` of any one meridian of it. (2) *The bounds near a cusp are the cusp's.* Within the first span the derivatives along the border are bounded to its order, `|S_σ| ≤ speed β^k`, `|S_σσ| ≤ bend β^k`, `|S_στ| ≤ twist β^(k−1)` (from `N`, the differences of its net and of the weights: `S_σσ = β^k (N_σ / w² − 2 N w_σ / w³)`, `S_στ` likewise with `k β^(k−1) N`), and a cell or a border edge there is judged by them at its far side (`Chart::bounds_in`) instead of by the bounds for the whole face: with those, the cells near the border would have to be as small across as the width is along, all around it (3.6e7 cells by an estimate; the first try stopped at the million-cell cap). (3) *A curve that ends on that border is drawn on to the point* along the intersection: at places across halving the distance to the pole, the zero of `f` along the border's parameter next to the last one, no further from it than `twist / speed` times the step across (the zero continues the last; past that it is refused), by bracketing and regula falsi on its sign alone (scale-free: `f` vanishes there as the width does; a place within `on` of the other surface is taken as it is, as `correct` takes one), until the place is within `on` of the pole or every iso-curve there is shorter than `on` (nearer than that the sign of `f` is rounding: a plane whose frame is 28 units away has `f` to 6e-15 where the needle is 1e-24 wide); then the point itself, with the direction the meridians leave it along (the derivative across, the same on all of them at a cusp). The points are refined as every curve's are, against both surfaces. Where the zero is lost on the way (the intersection turns back within the strip) it is refused by name ("comes close to a cusp of a surface and turns back before it"). Measured: the bounds against the needle and a cone sampled from a millionth of the span to all of it at three scales, the needle's iso-curve within a factor of 1.8 of its bound (`pole.rs` › `a_cusp_narrows_to_the_second_order_within_its_bounds`); the curves of three planes and three cylinders through the axis end on the point to the last bit and lie within their deviation of both surfaces at 400 places each, and a plane across the axis 0.003 from the point still cuts its circle (`crates/geom/tests/meet_cusp.rs`); `boolean_poles.rs` › `a_cusp_cut_along_its_axis_is_what_its_profile_says`: three planes through the axis and the cylinder, halves to `π ∫ ρ² dz / 2` and `(curved + lid) / 2 + 2 ∫ ρ dz` within 1e-7, the cylinder's common part to the lens of two discs integrated up the profile (1e-7), the identities of sets, 400 seeded points against the exact membership, every result valid, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6; `a_cusp_quartered_along_its_axis_…` the block edge, a quarter and its sections, at scale 1 (and at all three, turned: ignored as slow, green in 5.5 min in release). The cone (a pole of the first order, analytic and its spline twin) cut the same ways measures as its closed forms (`a_cone_cut_along_its_axis_is_what_it_measures`). Surfaces through the point that leave the axis (`surfaces_through_a_cusp_that_leave_its_axis_are_cut_or_refused`, three scales): the ball of radius `c` = 2e-3 and 3e-3 through the point is cut right (four faces; the common tip measures `π y⁵ / 80` up to `2 c`, 4.02e-14 at scale 1), a plane a hundredth of a radian off the axis is cut right, one a thousandth off is refused ("tangent or nearly coincident"), a cone about the axis with its apex on the point is refused as before ("tangent or nearly coincident over too large an area"); the chord's cylinder and ball through the point and the axis at 4e-3 are refused ("tangent or nearly coincident over too large an area", as before the cusp rule), and a ball of radius 1 tangent to the axis at 1e-3 is refused ("touch along a curve"); every result held to 400 seeded points, points along the axis next to the point and points 1.8 tolerances deep next to the chord's loop (points within half a tolerance of either surface not asked). The reviewer's probe of 54 tools (thin cylinders holding the axis, planes along the axis offset by 0.5 to 20 tolerances, planes through the point 1e-4 to 1e-2 off the axis, tangent balls, chords) at three scales, all three operations: no wrong body; all refused but two of the planes 1e-2 off the axis, which are right. The results are meshed since the same day ([`tessellator.md`](tessellator.md) › *Cuts along the axis of a cusp and of a spline cone*). *Open:* (a) at a tolerance tighter for the size (the needle ten times larger in a document of 1e-6 mm, 1.7e-8 of its height against the tests' 1.7e-7) the half by a block whose face holds the axis is refused ("surfaces that touch along a curve"; at 1e-5 to 1e-3 mm it is cut and meshed); (b) speed: the block's edge along the axis is within the tolerance of the needle for 2e-3 from the point, every point of the axis is as near to a whole circle of the needle; the time is in the lower bounds that split the ring around such a point, and less in the nearest-point searches that descend from every piece of it (measured 2026-10-17, *Speed of freeform booleans and of their measures* below: 47 to 86 s for the first boolean of each case before, 21 to 46 s after, the next ones on the same operands a few seconds; the planes take 30 to 120 ms); (c) a piece of the intersection inside the strip thinner than a tolerance across is not seen by `same_crossings`; the strip's width rests on the bound of the whole first span, which is looser on a surface whose spans are wide; the block edge at 1e-3, 1e6 and turned is held only in an ignored test.

**Poles at a corner of the parameters (2026-10-15, `boolean/freeform.rs` › `Corner`, `geom/src/pole.rs`, `geom/src/intersect`, `tess/src/face.rs` › `CornerPole`).** A patch whose borders `u = 0` and `v = 0` (or any border across u and one across v) are both one point closes into that point along an L of its parameter rectangle; the two other borders meet at an ordinary corner. A lune of a ball between two meridians is such a patch when it is the inverse stereographic projection from its north pole of the quadrant `X = (1 − s) / (s t)`, `Y = (1 − t) / (s t)`: a rational patch of bidegree (2, 2), raised to (3, 3) so that every weight is positive. Until now it was refused (`POLES_BOTH_WAYS`). With `s` and `t` the shares of the domain from the pole's borders:

- *The chart* (`Corner`). The point goes to `(s t (s − t), s t)` (its first coordinate negated when one of the two shares is measured backwards, so that it turns the way the parameters do): the L is the origin, `(s − t, s t)` is one to one on the square, and near the origin the chart is a wedge `|x| ≤ y` in which the meridians leave the pole in their order along the L. Its speed: with `τ = s t` and `σ = t − s` a step moves the chart by at most `√3 |(dτ, τ dσ)|`, and the surface by `A dτ + B τ dσ` with `A = (t G + s H) / (s + t)` and `B = (H − G) / (s + t)`, where `S_s = t G` and `S_t = s H` (the surface less the pole vanishes on both borders). `G` and `H` are bounded from the nets of `w² S_α` and `w² S_β` divided by the piece's own parameter where the piece reaches a border (`divided`, and `divided_first` for the first parameter), `H − G` from the net of `w² (t S_t − s S_s)` (products of nets with linear factors, `times_linear`) and `G × H` from the normal's net divided by both. At the corner itself `H − G` and `G × H` vanish as `s + t` does; there a field `X` that vanishes at the corner is written `α X_a + β X_b` (`over_sum`), and `X / (a α + b β)` lies in the hull of `X_a / a` and `X_b / b`. The bound is valid wherever the surface leaves the pole in a fan; on the lune it is 29 times the fastest the chart is seen to move, on a cone with its apex at a corner 4 to 10 times (`freeform.rs` › `the_chart_of_a_surface_with_poles_moves_no_faster_than_its_speed`, which now has the lune with its pole at each of the four corners and the cone).
- *The direction the surface closes around* (`corner_axis`): the mean of the limit normals along the whole L, the rows of the doubly divided normal net on both borders and, at the corner, of the two halves `over_sum` gives.
- *The side of the surface at the pole* (`Pole::normal_towards`, `normal_along`): the meridians leave the pole from both borders, and the nearest of all is taken; a meridian near the end of a border that is the other pole's border is looked at as far from that end as from its own border (nearer, both derivatives are rounding). The nearest point next to the pole (`field.rs`, `foot_from`) is looked for again from the meridian of every pole the search ended next to.
- *The surface intersections.* A surface through the pole makes the walked distance vanish as `s t` at the corner, a saddle just outside the chart around which no cell is simple down to the floor ("tangent or nearly coincident over too large an area"): a freeform surface with a corner pole is measured against rather than walked (`meet.rs` › `chart_rank`), where it is a surface like any other. Nodes of the projector's grid on the two borders are one point and are not compared for curvature.
- *The tessellator* (`CornerPole`): a boundary that comes into the pole goes along the L from the meridian of the point before (its smaller share dropped: near the border `t = 0` the line of constant `s` leaves the pole in the direction `R(s, 0)` of `S = P + s t R`) to that of the point after, round the corner when they are on different borders; the pole is also recognised by position (its projection may come back off the L). A stretch into or out of the corner is subdivided along whichever parameter moves (`finish_boundary`).

*Held to* (`crates/kernel/tests/boolean_corner_pole.rs`): a quarter of a ball (x and y positive) whose lune is that patch against the same booleans of the analytic quarter ball, with blocks, cylinders, balls, cones and tori away from the poles, around the north pole (the corner of the parameters), through it (on a block's face, a block's edge, the side of a ball and a cone) and around the south pole (the patch's ordinary corner), upright and tilted, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6: within 2.7e-7 in volume and area (held to 1e-6, as the octant), the identities of sets to 1e-7, every result valid, meshed wherever the analytic result meshes, and at scale 1 without a crossing of its own mesh (`body_self_intersections`); the pole at each of the other corners of the patch and with the parameters exchanged, cut through the pole. The twin itself measures as the closed forms (`π r³ / 3`, `2π r²`) to 1e-9 and meshes. *Refused:* a patch that closes into a point on two adjacent borders but leaves it along one border all in one direction (`u v R(v)`: no fan, `IRREGULAR` or `POLE_SHAPE`).

**Showcase findings (2026-10-15, `geom/src/intersect/march.rs`, `boolean/section.rs`, `topo/src/mass.rs`).** *A box that cuts a curve* (the lamp's shell of revolution less a union of ten lofted cells: "an intersection curve could not be fitted within the tolerance", though every nine of the cells and each alone passed). Every pair of faces is intersected inside the box both bodies share, so a union of ten cells gave the shell's wall and one cell a box the size of the lamp, and that box cut a branch of their curve. The marcher fitted the pieces as they were, so the span from the last point inside to the point on the box was a new cubic with a new end tangent that nobody held to the tolerance (1.05e-6 where the target is 0.5e-6). The pieces of a branch the box cut are now refined as the branch was. *Held to:* `crates/geom/tests/meet_fixes.rs` (the shade's wall, inlined, and the cell's end cap in the recorded box: every curve within the tolerance of both surfaces along its whole length, cut pieces included; refused before), `crates/engine/tests/boolean_findings.rs` (from the lamp's steps: the same, and the identities of the three booleans in place and turned to 1e-8), `crates/kernel/tests/boolean_boxes.rs` (two crossing spline roofs, tilted, at three scales, in 60 seeded boxes; it does not reproduce the lamp's case, which needs its surfaces). The boolean corpus gives the same counts (11573 agreeing, 249 refused, none wrong or invalid), digest `19af10963b6f12c2`.

*Outlines when needed* (`boolean/section.rs` › `Lazy`, `classify.rs`). The lamp's torus bead united with a shade after 111 cuts and 110 fillets took 6.8 GB (7.2 GB measured with the shade's regeneration, 1.4 GB without the bead) and 1 s on the plain shade: preparing the operands made the outline of every face, and the outline of a freeform face indexes its surface (`PatchTree`: up to 2^15 pieces, tens of megabytes), 5.8 GB for faces of which few are near the bead. A face's box and hull are taken from its loops and its surface without the outline (`outline::hull_of`; a cone asks the outline whether the face reaches the apex), and its outline is made the first time the boolean needs it (a pair of faces whose boxes and hulls meet, a ray that meets its box, a face cut or near the other body's box), once. An outline is a function of the face alone, so the result is the same; a face that cannot be outlined is refused when it is needed, not because it is in the operand. Measured: the bead's union peaks at 2.2 GB, of which 0.85 GB are the faces along the rim (13.4 min with the shade's regeneration, 16.6 before).

*A face with many holes* (`topo::mass`, the lamp's finding M1: the shade with six cells cut through it, "a boundary integral that does not settle"). The face has eight loops; its whole boundary, refined together, needs 1.04 budgets of work though every loop settles at its second sum: one fixed budget does not fit faces of every size. The boundary integral is a sum of one term per sample, so it is the sum of one integral per piece of the boundary, and each piece is refined on its own until two of its sums in a row agree, and not summed again once it has (on a surface closed both ways the parts are the loops, unrolled whole; through two poles, and on a plane bounded by lines and circles, the boundary goes together as before). Each part is held to its own allowance, rounding included, and the face to their sum, as its sum carries the rounding of each part; a part's rounding is that of the face's reach (its sum is of inner integrals that run across the face to it), which measures right: held to the part's own reach a fillet's face of six parts did not settle, and one allowance shared out over the face is tighter than the parts' sums can be (a torus at scale 1e-3 did not settle). The work is bounded for the face: its first sums are as much work as its boundary at its coarsest, up to a ceiling of sixteen budgets (`FIRST_SUMS_WORK`, refused past it as any face that does not settle); in all the face may take 64 times what those took (`PARTS_WORK`: every part to its third sum and some further), or the fixed budget where that is more. Measured: the shade's face of eight holes takes 1.04 budgets of a cap of 14.4; the bead shade's faces of 48 and 53 holes have first sums of 2.3 and 2.4 budgets and take 10.7 and 11.2 (4.6 times their first sums) of caps of 149 and 155. With the second moments summed beside, the sums without them are taken per part once those agree, as before for the whole face. *Held to:* `crates/engine/tests/boolean_findings.rs` (the shade from its steps, measured, and what is left and what was taken make the shell to 1e-8), `topo::mass` tests (a cap whose whole boundary needs more than its budget is measured part by part with half of it, and a face whose part through the sphere's other pole never settles stops at the cap, with at most one round on top), `crates/kernel/tests/mass_many_holes.rs` (a spline roof with thirty holes, at three scales and turned, against an integral the test sums itself, to 1e-7).

*One meeting of two edges* (`boolean/section.rs` › `one_meeting`, the impeller's finding F8: blades with a rounded trailing edge on a hub of revolution, "two points closer together than the tolerance can tell apart"). Where the edge of a blade's fillet runs onto the hub's cylinder at a shallow angle right at the hub's rim, the place where it meets the rim's circle is found twice: as the circle crossing the fillet's surface and as the fillet's edge crossing the cylinder. Each edge is within the tolerance of the curve it stands for, so at a shallow angle the two places are up to the tolerance over the sine of the angle apart (6.6e-5 at a tolerance of 1e-4, beyond the half tolerance within which points merge). Two such places, the one found on an edge of A and the other on an edge of B, each within the tolerance of both edges, are one vertex: the cuts along a curve and the vertices of the section take them as one. *Held to:* `crates/kernel/tests/boolean_edge_meeting.rs` (a block whose edge lies 0.4 tolerances off its face and a block whose edge crosses it with a face at 0.35 to 0.45 rad to it, the two places 0.65 to 0.85 tolerances apart, at three scales: refused before, now the three booleans and their identities to the tolerance over the area), `crates/engine/tests/boolean_findings.rs` (two such blades on the hub from their steps, the union, and the identities in place and turned). The corpus gives the same results bit for bit (digest `19af10963b6f12c2`); 21 of its 249 refusals, cases built a tolerance apart, now refuse one step later, as two faces between half and twice the tolerance apart.

*The box of the two faces (2026-10-15, `boolean/section.rs`, `geom/src/intersect/march.rs` › `united`, `topo/src/mass.rs` › `Outline::noise`).* The impeller's pattern of two blades with a rounded leading edge, apart from each other (finding F7), was refused as "tangent or nearly coincident over too large an area": the fillet's surface of one blade and the side of the other run tangent far from both faces, inside the box the bodies share. A blade with a rounded leading edge on a hub of revolution (F5) was refused as "two curves leave a point in the same direction". Each pair of faces is now intersected inside the box the two faces share, grown by 16 tolerances and 1.3 % of the diagonal of the larger face's box (the room the box of the bodies had, taken from the faces; from the overlap of the two face boxes instead, half a drop cut by a plane did not tessellate, its curve cut off too close to the face): the faces are all the boolean asks about, and a far lump in a tool changes nothing. Two things the smaller box exposed near a pole, each a general fault the old boxes happened not to meet:

- *Two traces of one branch* (`march.rs` › `united`; the octant cut through its pole by a block's edge at 1e-3, `regions::untie`: "two curves leave a point in the same direction"). The face of the block through the pole holds the tangent of the patch's border there, so their curve leaves the pole along the border, within `y² / 10R` of it: within the tolerance of the border for `√(10 R tol)` (4500 tolerances at 1e-3). The march came onto the border somewhere along that stretch (where is rounding: the border is within the marcher's own precision of the other surface there) and stopped, 3.8 tolerances from the pole; a seed between that end and the pole was further from the end than "just past it" (half a tolerance) and started a second trace of the whole branch, which the boolean took as a second curve from the same far vertex, one to the pole and one to a vertex 3.8 tolerances from it on the border; at the pole the two arcs could not be put in order at any distance. Now two traces of one branch are one: when the quarter points of either lie on the other within the tolerance, the first keeps what the second reaches beyond either of its ends, and an end of the first that the second runs past is left out (it was the border's point, not the curve's: kept, it touches the face's other edge, and the tessellator could not mesh the sliver between them). A seed past an end is no longer the only way to know a branch was found.
- *What a boundary is known to* (`topo::mass`). With the curves this box gives, the measure of the corner-pole tests' patch face (`boolean_corner_pole.rs`, a block's edge through the pole) no longer settled: a part of the boundary through the pole, its edge within 1e-9 of the patch, scattered at 2e-9 from one doubling to the next, sizes included, because the parameters of the feet turn around the pole with where the edge is found. A boundary is known on its surface only as well as its edges lie on it: the furthest a sample of the part summed lies from its foot counts as the boundary's rounding where it is more, the allowance being that over how wide the part is, as for rounding. For edges that lie on their surfaces (every exact one) nothing changes.

*Held to:* `crates/kernel/tests/boolean_poles.rs` › `a_plane_along_the_border_at_the_pole_meets_the_patch_once` (the patch and that plane in 40 seeded boxes about the pole, upright and tilted, at three scales: one curve, ending at the pole or where it is still within the tolerance of the border, on the circle the plane cuts from the ball to the tolerance; two curves without `united`), `a_patch_with_a_pole_is_cut_through_the_pole` (refused with the face box alone), `boolean_corner_pole.rs` (unsettled without the allowance), `crates/engine/tests/boolean_findings.rs` (the impeller's pattern is twice the blade in faces and area, its volume within the tolerance over the area; the hub's union, difference and intersection satisfy the identities within that), `boolean_boxes.rs` › `a_far_block_in_the_tool_changes_nothing` (the difference of two crossing spline roofs, with a far block added to the tool and without, to 1e-9 at three scales).

*The box of a face on a torus (2026-10-15, `boolean/outline.rs` › `hull_of`, `section.rs` › `clip`; the bike's finding B6).* Two seat stays, each a tube of 6 mm swept along an arc of a circle of 2.8 m (a face on a torus), one the mirror image of the other with 10 mm between them, were refused as one: "an intersection curve could not be fitted". A face's box comes from what it lies within the convex hull of, and that counted a face on a torus as the ball around the whole torus and an arc as its whole circle: both faces had boxes of 5.5 m, so had the pair, and their tori, mirror images, meet far from both faces, where the curve of two tubes that cross at the mirror plane was not fitted. Now a face on a torus counts as the tube around the stretch of its central circle that it turns through about the axis: the balls of the tube's radius about the polygon of that stretch (its pieces of at most an eighth of a turn and the corners where their end tangents meet, which stand out by under 2 % of the radius), cut to the ball around the whole torus; both hold the face and its boundary. A face whose outline cannot be made keeps the ball, as before. The stretch is found from the boundary: each piece lies within the hull of its points (an arc within such a polygon of its own), and where those stay within less than half a turn of each other about the axis so does the piece; between the stretches the boundary takes, a circle of the tube has no boundary on it and is all in the face or all out of it, and one point of it says which (as the apex of a cone does). A face that turns all the way round, or whose boundary cannot be placed so, keeps the ball. The two stays' boxes then do not meet, and the pair is not intersected at all. Smaller boxes cut more curves, and a cut end lies clear of one of the two face boxes: a stretch between two cuts crosses the boundary of neither face (where it would, an edge meets the other surface, and that is a cut), so it is out of a face wherever any point of it is, and a stretch with an end clear of either box is now left out without classifying its middle (the fillet of a cambered blade's tip at 1e-3, a torus 5e-5 thick, put the middle of such a stretch 6e-5 beyond the face's end, where the winding number about it did not settle). *Held to:* `crates/kernel/tests/boolean_boxes.rs` › `two_bent_tubes_whose_tori_meet_far_away_are_apart` (two tubes of 6 mm on a ring of 2776 mm, mirrored, 1.6 mm apart at their nearest, their tori crossing the mirror plane 0.2 rad further round: union both, intersection empty, difference the first, by Pappus, upright and tilted, at three scales; refused before), the bike's reproducers `two-stays-union.json` and `two-stays-union-arc.json` (`target/work/bike/findings`).

*The piece of a curve an edge is (2026-10-16, `boolean/mod.rs` › `pieces_of_splines`).* An edge cut from a fitted curve, or a piece of an operand's spline edge split by the section, kept the whole curve with a range, so the hull of every face it bounds (which counts a spline's control points) reached as far as the branch: the turbofan's casing cut by a row of holes through a cone kept every hole's whole loop with each piece of it, 222 control points reaching 5 mm past edges half a millimetre long, and each later boolean met 72 more pairs of faces whose boxes only seemed to meet (the benchmark's 45873 pairs against 42372; once a pair is intersected in the box of its two faces, that box is grown from those hulls). Every such edge of the result now gets its piece of the curve, cut out exactly by knot insertion (`geom::refine::piece`), when the body is made: after `merge`, which joins two edges of one curve by its identity. The count is back to 42369 (work's 42372; triangles 95331). *Held to:* `boolean_boxes.rs` › `an_edge_cut_from_a_fitted_curve_is_its_piece_of_it` (the three booleans of two crossing spline roofs at three scales: every spline edge runs over its whole curve, its control points within the edge's length of it), and the benchmark's count of face pairs.

*Open: a fitted curve depends on the box it was marched in (2026-10-16).* The marcher's step comes from the box it is asked in (`hmax` = the box's diagonal / 32, the first step a quarter of that), so the same two surfaces marched in another box give another fit of the same curve, within the same tolerance. Downstream results that hang on more than the tolerance promises then move: intersecting each pair of faces in its own box moved the sliver of `crates/testkit/tests/mass_sliver.rs` (a slab 1e-5 thick) by 2.2e-9 from the direct integral, where the fit in the box of the bodies happened to land 6.6e-10 from it, and it pushed the tessellator's search for a seam of the drilled pipe (`winding_seam_solids.rs`) past a cap of 256 tries (removed). The general method owed: a step rule from the curve and the tolerance alone (its curvature and the turn allowed), so that a curve is the same curve whatever box asked for it.

**Coincident freeform faces (2026-10-15, `boolean/hits.rs`, `geom/src/intersect/march.rs` › `lies_on`).** Two refusals, two causes. *An edge that lies on a patch for a while and leaves it where the patch ends* (the rational quarter rod against its analytic twin lifted along the axis: the lifted body's straight edge, from 1 to 4, lies on the twin's patch, which ends at 3): the distance along the edge is zero over the stretch on the patch and then grows, and the root isolation took the stretch for a touch somewhere along it, narrowing in on a distance that is zero all along; the cut it made (z = 1.0278 on an edge from 1 to 4) was a vertex where nothing is, and the stretch along the edge no longer fitted the edge's pieces ("an imprinted curve that does not fit the edges it should coincide with"). Now a stretch of the curve within a quarter of the tolerance of a surface that ends (a freeform one), longer than 64 tolerances, is cut where it comes onto the surface and where it leaves it (the last place within a quarter of the tolerance, by bisection towards the clear stretch beside it), as touches (a ray that runs along a face is still no ray to count by). *A patch on a patch that ends sooner* (the quarter rod against itself lifted): one surface lies on another when 81 samples of it are within the tolerance of the other; samples past the end of a freeform one were among them, so a patch was never found on a patch it overhangs, and the marcher refused the tangency along the whole face ("surfaces that touch along a curve … without a closed form"). Samples whose nearest point of a freeform surface is on its border are now left out, and at least 16 of the 81 must count. *Held to* (`boolean_rational.rs` › `a_rational_face_on_the_surface_it_equals_is_right`): the quarter rod as a rational patch against its analytic twin lifted, against itself lifted, against a patch a fifth larger of itself lifted and the larger patch against itself lifted, upright and tilted, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, against the same booleans of the analytic rods to 1e-6, every result valid and meshed.

**Freeform faces that close on themselves (2026-10-14, `boolean/chart.rs`, `geom/src/closure.rs`, `geom/src/intersect`).** A Bézier or NURBS surface whose control net ends on the row it starts with, on clamped knots (`geom::closure::closes`, the test the measure and the tessellator take: a vase wall, a NURBS cylinder, ball or torus as a STEP file brings them, with or without a seam edge), was refused by the boolean wherever a cut crossed its seam ("a piece of a face is not closed off by its boundary"): its chart was the parameter rectangle, in which a loop over the seam is not closed, and the surface intersections walked it as a rectangle too, ending curves at the seam and, past it, on the polynomial the end span continues in (a curve that came back on itself). Two places read such a surface through its parameters, and both now know it closes:

- *The chart* (`chart.rs`, `Layout`). With `s` the share of the way around and `t` of the way across, the way around is an angle `θ = 2π s`: closed one way, the annulus `(1 + t) e^{iθ}`; with a pole at one end, the disc `t e^{iθ}` (or `(1 − t)`), the pole its centre; with a pole at each end (a ball), `z = t e^{iθ} / (1 − t)` puts one pole at the origin and the other at infinity and `w = 1 / (z − z₀)` sends a point `z₀` clear of the loops to infinity instead (the poles and twelve places between them are tried, the one furthest from the loops taken; refused within 1e-3 of the surface's size), so both poles are ordinary points and the face may contain the point left out, as on a sphere; closed both ways (a torus), cut open along a line of constant `t` (or `s`) that the loops do not cross, found as for the analytic torus by following each boundary piece through the nearest point, and `(1 + (t − cut mod 1)) e^{iθ}`. The direction between two images is never computed through a far point: for the ball, `(a_q b_p − a_p b_q) conj(A_p) conj(A_q)` with `a = t e^{iθ}`, `b = 1 − t`, `A = a b₀ − a₀ b`, bounded and continuous through the pole at infinity of `z`. The speed is bounded from the tree's bound on the Jacobian: in the shares, `4π` times it for the annulus and the torus (`r ≤ 2`), `√(1 + 4π²)` and `√(4 + 4π²)` times the wedge's bound (`wedge_sigma`, `ω` the width that vanishes at a pole) for the disc and the ball, where the derivatives of the direction along `s` vanish as `ω` does. Measured, the bounds are 4 to 13 times the fastest the chart is seen to move, across the seam too (`chart.rs` › `the_chart_of_a_closed_surface_moves_no_faster_than_its_speed`). Refused by name: a surface that closes into a point along the same parameter it closes in, or closes both ways and into a point (`CLOSED_POLE`); a torus-like face whose boundary crosses every line both ways (`NO_SEAM_CUT`); a ball-like face whose boundary comes near every point tried (`NO_DROP`).
- *The surface intersections* (`intersect/field.rs`, `march.rs`). Such a surface is a periodic chart, as a cylinder is: the quadtree wraps its cells, and a branch is marched over the seam with its parameters taken around into the domain where the surface is evaluated (`Chart::at`, and the start of the projector's search). A curve around the surface closes, and one across the seam is one curve. A border across the way around that lies on the other surface (a rim on a plate) is reported as a closed curve; one that is a pole gives none.

Besides: a face without loops on a surface closed both ways, or one way with a pole at each end, is the whole surface, as on a sphere or a torus (merge may join pieces into one, `freeform::borderless`); the fixed points looked at inside such a face are taken in the shares of the domain. *Held to* (`crates/kernel/tests/boolean_closed_twins.rs`): a NURBS can (with and without a seam edge, the seam in two places, the turn in u or v), ball, cup and torus, each one face on a rational surface that closes, against the same solid on the analytic cylinder, sphere and torus, with blocks, cylinders, balls, cones and tori that cross the seam, cut through both poles, around the axis and across the tube; the three booleans at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, in the world's frame and tilted: 594 booleans, all built and valid, within 7.9e-8 of the analytic twin in volume and area (held to 1e-6), the identities of sets to 2.4e-8 (held to 1e-7), and `(A − B) ∪ (A ∩ B)` built and measured as A. Six results through both poles of a ball cut along its seam are not measured by `topo` (named there) and are held to their twins by 4000 sampled points each; 77 results are declined by the tessellator (listed by the test). The vase of `boolean_closed_freeform.rs` (a Bézier profile turned, the seam inside the half, on the cut and outside it) is cut in half at three scales, within 9.1e-9 of half its volume. *Cost:* 0.13 to 0.4 s per boolean (the test profile), as for other rational faces. *Not done:* knots that are not clamped (a closure the net does not show).

**The helmet's findings (2026-10-15, `geom/src/intersect/field.rs`, `march.rs`, `geom/src/pole.rs`, `boolean/section.rs`).** A shell turned from two splines and cut by lofted frustums (the Voronoi helmet; `crates/kernel/tests/boolean_helmet.rs`) found six mistakes, all where two freeform faces meet. Five are in how a freeform surface on the *field* side of the marcher (the surface measured against, not the one walked) was read: a lofted side whose corners are rounded is a bilinear patch, not a plane, so the shell is the field (with a planar tool the shell is walked and none of these happen). The sixth is in the bound below the distance to a freeform face:

- *Curvature at a pole.* The projector estimates the curvature of a freeform surface from how fast the normal turns between neighbouring nodes of its 25 × 25 grid. Nodes on one pole are one point, apart by rounding (1e-14 at a size of 100), each with the limit normal of its own meridian; their difference over that distance read as a curvature of 1e7 per unit length, every cell within two grid steps of the pole was too bent to be simple, and the boolean refused "tangent or nearly coincident over too large an area". Nodes on one pole are no longer compared.
- *The nearest point next to a pole.* Newton's step for the nearest point cannot turn from one meridian to another at a pole (the derivative along its border is zero), and next to it the step along the border is ill-conditioned: from the pole node, or by a step that clamps onto the border, it kept to the meridian it started on and stopped at the pole or on that meridian, for a point a millimetre off the pole a millimetre from the surface. A search that ends within one grid row of a pole is done again from the meridian that leaves the pole towards the point (`Pole::meridian_towards`), first along that meridian, then in both parameters, and the nearer foot is kept.
- *Pieces between the field's borders.* Past a border of a freeform surface the field goes on (the distance to the nearest point, on the border), and the quadtree sees only that: a piece of curve between two borders of the field (a frustum's side cutting a corner off a half of the shell, between the half's meridian and the vent's rim) lay in simple cells whose every edge seed was past the field's edge, and was lost ("imprint lost"). The marcher now also seeds where a border of a freeform field that is an edge (not a seam, not a pole) crosses the walked surface: along each such border, bisection on its parameter wherever the distance to the walked surface could be zero (it changes no faster than the border moves; the finest piece is sixteen tolerances, and at most 4096 pieces per border), and a root that is on the walked surface gives a seed.
- *A pole border is no curve.* A border of the walked patch that lies on the other surface is reported as a curve; a border that is one point (the pole of an octant on an open patch) was reported as a curve from the pole to itself, a few tolerances long in the chart's parameters, which on the other face hid the arc that divides it. It was already left out where the border runs around a closed surface; now everywhere.
- *A loop through a cut place.* An end of a curve next to a place where an edge meets a surface is that place, and the place is found on the curve once. A curve that begins and ends there (the figure eight in which a torus meets the plane it touches at its inner equator, through the octant's pole) had the place at its start only, and the lobe back into the face was dropped. An end whose place was found at the other end of the curve is now that place itself.

- *A bound below the distance that is one* (`boolean/freeform.rs`, `PatchTree::bound_by` and `refine`). Asked up to a threshold (does the distance exceed it?), the bound stopped as soon as the nearest piece looked at so far was under the threshold and returned that piece's distance; a nearer piece not yet looked at could undercut it, so between the threshold (1.001 times what is asked) and what is asked the value was no bound. A ray through a lofted frustum was proven clear of the side it leaves by (its distance there 0, the value returned 1.0006 tolerances), a face of another frustum, apart from it, was taken for inside and left out, and the union of the two had edges used once (the check caught it: "produced an invalid body"; the helmet's frustums from 60 mm, united). The pieces left (the heap's nearest, the boxes of those still to be split) now count. The union of radial frustums that come within a few millimetres of each other towards the centre is no tolerance case: the same mistake.

With the fourth and fifth, the octant against a torus through its pole, tilted, at 1e6 (pinned as refused in `boolean_poles.rs`) is built and agrees with the analytic octant. *Held to:* four cells of the helmet against the shell with a pole and with a vent, the three booleans at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, valid, the identities of sets to 1e-7, and the common part against the same boolean with the cell's planar twin (its far corners the near ones scaled exactly, so that its sides are planes and the shell is walked instead) to 2e-5 (the twin's sides are 1e-5 mm off the cell's); two frustums apart, whose union is both (to 1e-12), whose intersection is empty and whose difference is the first; through the CLI, each of 70 cells cut from the shell with a pole, 71 from 60 mm and 71 from 100 mm from the shell with a vent, all built (before: 8, 1 and 1 refused), and the helmet's 71 frustums from 60 mm united and taken from the shell, valid (before: an invalid body). Unit tests in `field.rs` for the curvature and the nearest point next to a pole.

**Not done:** freeform faces whose parametrisation stalls anywhere but at a border that is one point; a lens between a freeform surface and another; speed (a boolean of a domed block and a block takes about 0.1 s, most of it nearest-point searches); a spatial index over the faces of a body (every face pair is still tested by box and by side); an untouched operand does not come back with its entities in their old order.

### The boolean's own check (2026-10-17)

The third oracle the developer asked for on 2026-10-17 (roadmap › *Oracles owed*): a cheap check after every boolean, beside `topo::check`, that the result is the boolean of its operands and not just a valid solid. `kernel::boolean::verify(a, b, op, result, tolerance, options)`; every rule holds for any two solids and any surface, and each allowance comes from the tolerance or from the measurement, none is tuned.

| Rule | Allowance, and why it is general |
|---|---|
| **Volumes.** Union: `max(V(A), V(B)) ≤ V(R) ≤ V(A) + V(B)`. Difference: `V(A) − V(B) ≤ V(R) ≤ V(A)`. Intersection: `V(A) + V(B) − V(H) ≤ V(R) ≤ min(V(A), V(B))`, `H` the box around both. `V(R) ≥ 0`. | The error bounds of the three volumes, which `topo::mass_bound` reports (new: the sum of what the integrator held each of its sums to, `1e-12` of their size plus their rounding, per face, part, panel group and whole surface, and the rounding of adding the faces), plus `2 t (S(A) + S(B))`: what the boolean decides within the tolerance `t` (coincident, touching, one vertex) lies within `t` of the boundary of an operand, and the band of half-width `t` around a surface of area `S` has volume `2 t S` to first order. The next terms (`π t²` times the edge length, `t³` per vertex) are smaller by the ratio of `t` to a face's width and are left out. A body that cannot be measured leaves the rule unchecked (`unmeasured`), never a violation. |
| **Reach.** Every vertex of R and the middle of every edge lies within `t` of the box of A or of B (union), of A (difference), of A and of B (intersection). | `t`: vertices are merged within half of it, and the boxes are conservative (`Body::face_bbox`). |
| **Out of the tool's reach the target is unchanged.** A face of A whose box is more than `t` from B's box is in R (union, difference), and so is a face of B far from A (union): a face of R on the same surface (equal, or within `t` of it at the face's boundary points with the same outward normal), on the same side, with as many loops and edges, each edge's ends and middle within `t`. For an intersection such a face is outside R, which the reach rule checks. | `t` for "far" and for each matched point. The face's box holds the face, so the face is outside the other operand; the boolean never splits it (no curve can meet it) and merges only pieces of one face or coincident faces of both, and keeps the vertices an operand had. Found through the result's edges by their middles (sorted along x), so it costs a box per operand face and no box per result face. |
| **Membership.** At 16 seeded points (SplitMix64, a fixed seed; half in the box around both, half where the boxes overlap), in R exactly when the set formula of in A and in B says so. | Points within `2 t` of a boundary are passed over (`contains_points` says nothing there); a body the ray casting does not take is reported (`unclassified`), never a violation. |

**Where it runs.** (a) Inside every boolean, rules *reach* and *far faces* (`VerifyOptions::ALWAYS`); a violation is `KernelError::WrongResult` with the first violation and their count. (b) In full in the tests: `boolean_common::solve`, `solve_parts` and `verified` (most boolean test files), `boolean_random`, and every result of the corpus (a violation is an `INVALID` outcome). (c) In full in `document.check` deep, for every boolean feature in the history (its body taken by a later feature or not), problems named by feature and, for a far face, by the operand face's persistent name; `booleansChecked` counts them. (d) In full in every boolean of a process with `CRAFT3D_BOOLEAN_CHECK=full` (a sweep of a whole test suite, fillets and shells included; for test runs only: a boolean may then be refused in one process and not in another, so no document may depend on it).

**Cost, and why only part of it always** (release, the five bench models, 1196 booleans, 46.6 s of booleans; CPU time beside the boolean's own): reach and far faces 3.9 % (face boxes 2.6 %, the matching 0.9 %, reach 0.3 %); with the 16 points 56 %; with the volumes 690 % (three mass integrations of bodies of hundreds of faces against a boolean that looks only where the tool is). Below the 5 % the developer set, the first two run always; the points and volumes are left to (b)–(d). The first matching cost 27 % on the plate with 500 holes: a far face's candidates include the plate's top face with all its loops, whose edges were evaluated for every hole; loops and edges are now counted before anything is evaluated (2.8 %).

**Found.** Nothing, over: the 1196 booleans of the bench models (every rule, before the decision); the kernel's test suite (546 tests) with the full check in the helpers and the always-on part in every boolean, and again in release with `CRAFT3D_BOOLEAN_CHECK=full` (every boolean of every operation, fillets, chamfers, shells and face operations included); the corpus, every one of its 11,988 outcomes held to the full check (digest `3f00bc8ced79a654` unchanged, 11,582 agree, 240 refused, none invalid); and 1338 boolean features of 131 showcase files of `target/work` (the weld table and the laser box among them) and the four bench scripts through `document.check` deep (the three lamp files took longer than the sweep's 15 minutes each and are not in it).

**Held to** (`crates/kernel/tests/boolean_check.rs`, at three scales): correct results of five pairs (plate and rod, cavity, overlapping blocks, ball and ring, rod and cone) pass every rule with an allowance below 1e-4 of the target's volume; seeded mistakes are caught: a far face of the target dropped (also by the always-on part), the union passed off as the difference (subtraction adds volume, out of reach, a point), the difference as the intersection and as the union, a far face's surface moved by ten tolerances, one of its vertices moved, the face turned inside out, an edge cut short, a cavity filled (the target returned as the target less a ball inside it: only a point tells), and a target grown by two allowances (subtraction adds volume). `topo`'s `the_mass_bound_covers_the_error_and_is_small`: the bound covers the error against the closed forms of the reference and trimmed solids and stays below 1e-9 of the volume.

**Open.** The far-face rule matches by box distance only: faces near the tool but outside it are not checked; the volume allowance leaves out the second-order terms of the band; a rule for the area (`S(R) ≤ S(A) + S(B)` holds, a lower bound does not) is not used.

### Speed of freeform booleans and of their measures (2026-10-17)

Profiled in release (stack samples of the running process, `eu-stack`, a few thousand per model; work counters where the samples left a doubt), on the showcase models opened (regenerated), measured, inspected and exported to STEP, the cusp's block edge and the boolean corpus. Where the time went before:

| Model | Where the time went |
|---|---|
| Helmet | 56 % the chart's speed near a stretch of a boundary, for the winding numbers (`PatchTree::sigma_near` › `finer` › `fine_piece`): in one boolean 874,000 queries, 11.2 million looks at a leaf and **36.7 million fine pieces made** (five Jacobians each) for a store of 16,384 that was emptied whole 2,960 times; section 38 %, classification 30 %, projections 21 %, checks 13 % |
| Lamp | 47 % **the sample grid of a numeric projection** (`Surface::project_numeric`: 289 evaluations of the surface for every point projected, the same 289 for every point of every boundary a tessellation or a mass integral projects); 18 % `topo::check` (regeneration checked again every body the boolean that made it had checked); about 37 % the tessellation for the self-intersection check, 28 % mass integration |
| Turbofan | 59 % mass integration: `document.measure` (34 %) and **the STEP export's shells** (24 %: 431 shells in 37 bodies, each measured on its own to tell a part from a cavity, one after the other, most with too few faces for a second thread) |
| Cusp, block edge along the axis | 84 % **lower bounds** (`PatchTree::bound_by`, splitting pieces in four: 6.6 million refine steps and 5.8 million quarters from 1,100 queries in the first boolean; points of the axis 1.0011e-6 from the needle against a threshold of 1.001e-6 split every piece of the ring around them to the budget of 32,768 steps per leaf, for an answer that is the threshold); nearest points 12 % (not the ties alone, as written before) |
| Corpus | spread over the same |

**What changed** (every answer the one the code gave before, bit for bit; each rule is a function of the inputs alone, for any surface):

- *A projection's samples are kept with the surface* (`Surface::project_grid`, `GridCache` in `NurbsSurface` and `BezierPatch`): the 17 × 17 samples, the extent, which directions close and which borders are one point are a function of the surface's numbers, made the first time a point is projected and cloned with the surface; equal surfaces stay equal and their debug text is unchanged. Held: projections onto a kept grid, onto a clone and onto a fresh surface are the same bits at three scales.
- *Remembered by the tree, by the bits of what is asked*: `sigma_near` (a winding number about every one of many points halves each piece of a boundary the same way and asks the same chart speed of each stretch; the speed does not depend on the point wound about) and `lower_bound` (root isolation asks again at the ends and quarters of the stretches it halves). Both are pure functions of their arguments (`finer`'s answer does not depend on what it kept), as `nearest`'s memo already was; each holds at most 16,384 answers and is emptied when full (`MAX_MEMO`, as `nearest`'s). What a tree remembers (the three memos, up to about 2.3 MB, and its fine pieces, up to about 11 MB for a bicubic surface) is counted in the shelf's budget with the tree itself (768 MB native, 128 MB in a browser, for all the trees on the shelf together), measured again each time a tree is asked for (`PatchTree::bytes`, `keep_within_budget`): before, only the nodes were counted, and 1,024 trees could have held about 2.3 GB of memos beside them.
- *The fine pieces share their corners*: a piece's quarters and halves have their corners among its corners, its middle and the middles of its sides, at the same parameters bit for bit; 8 Jacobians make four quarters instead of 20 (`fine_piece_with`). A full store lets go of the leaves asked about longest ago instead of emptying itself (no measurable gain on the helmet beside the shared corners; kept because the queries come along the curves of a boundary).
- *De Casteljau in place*: splitting a row, a patch along u or v and evaluating a Bézier patch no longer make a vector per level; `Slab::around` reads the control points without a vector of them. The arithmetic is the same, in the same order (held against the algorithm as it was written first, bit for bit).
- *Many bodies measured as runs of faces* (`topo::mass_properties_each`, each body's answer `mass_properties`'s; runs of at least 256 faces, a count of faces and not of threads, and none after the first body that fails, as asking one by one and stopping there would leave them): the STEP export's shells, and `document.measure`, `document.inspect`, the export's automatic tolerance and the scene's size ahead of their body-by-body questions (`engine::mass::measure_ahead`; bodies with a material are measured with their inertia as before). The faces' sums are added per body in face order, so the bits do not depend on the threads.
- *Checks remembered across regeneration* (`topo::check_remembered`, moved from the boolean): regeneration checks every feature's body, and a body the boolean that made it checked (written out in full with the tolerance, every bit) is not checked again.

Tried and left out: keeping the pieces `bound_by` splits (the same quarters for every query) gave 4 % on the cusp with room for 65,536 pieces and 27 % with a million (665 MB); not worth the memory.

**Proof of no change.** The boolean corpus gives the same digest, `3f00bc8ced79a654`, and the same counts (10,815 agreeing and 767 refused by design); the engine benchmark the same work counts, every one; the STEP files of the impeller, the turbofan, the helmet, the lamp and the bike are the same bytes (but for the file's name in the header), and their `document.measure` and `document.inspect` the same text.

**Before and after** (release, this machine, each pair run at the same time so that both see the same load; open, measure, inspect, export STEP; seconds wall / CPU):

| | Before | After | Load |
|---|---|---|---|
| Impeller (whole) | 99 / 122 | 91 / 104 | 20 |
| Helmet | 253 / 260 | 115 / 120 | 13–15 |
| Turbofan | 255 / 475 | 187 / 420 | 15–25 |
| Lamp | 2050 / 3507 | 1013 / 1508 | 30–60 |
| Bike (`s9`) | 176 / 164 | 152 / 135 | 21–24 |
| Cusp block edge, three scales and turned (18 booleans) | 823 / 681 | 326 / 244 | 40–60 |
| Its first boolean of each case | 47–86 | 21–46 | |
| Boolean corpus (back to back) | 554 / 1205 | 325 / 936 | 44–56 |
| Bench models, CPU (turbofan, engine block, impeller's first 82 steps, rotor, holes) | 36.3, 16.0, 39.4, 4.0, 14.6 | 34.0, 15.9, 32.8, 3.1–5.9, 11.7 | ~25 |

**Open.** The cusp's block edge still takes 20 to 45 s for its first boolean: the lower bounds quarter pieces of the needle that are long along the axis and a millionth around it, so that the ring's pieces double around at every level while only the axis needed halving; splitting a piece across its longer side only would need a fraction of the pieces, but gives other bounds (valid, not the same bits), so it is owed with its own proof that no decision changes. The nearest-point searches that tie around the axis (a third of what is left there) cannot be pruned without changing which of the equally near points is found. The meshes the self-intersection check makes are made again for every feature, face by face, where most faces are what they were (a face's mesh depends on its edges' points, which depend on both faces of the edge). Mass integration, now most of what is left of the turbofan, projects every boundary point with a walk from the last; it is the integrand's own work.

**Second round (2026-10-18): measures, projections, the self-check's meshes.** Profiled the same way (and `examples/measure_profile.rs`, new: the cost of `document.measure` apart from regenerating, with a digest of its answer). Where the time went at the start of the round: mass integration of the turbofan's measure was the boundary's projections (60 %: `Follow::project`, the walk of `Surface::project_from`) and the inner integrals (38 %); across everything, evaluating splines was the hottest code (`surface_at` and `Along::new`, the blend factors, 45 % of the samples of their own). In the lamp the self-check after every feature was 46 % of regeneration, 95 % of its boundaries in `Surface::project`, and most of those points had been projected onto the same surface the feature before (27 % found again with 4,096 kept per surface, 87 % with 32,768). Every change below gives the very bits the code gave before:

- *A line of a spline evaluated once per line* (`NurbsSurface::at_v`, `at_u`): along a line of constant v every row of control points is collapsed along v once (where the rows are no more than one panel's nodes would collapse on their own), along a line of constant u the blend factors along u are worked out once; the inner integrals of the mass properties evaluate their nodes that way.
